[Page 3]
3
Stay constructive into year end: earnings remain a tailwind on strong activity 
momentum, bond markets to be contained as inflation does not get de-anchored…
◼
Global equities are up 15% so far ytd, TR in USD, and we stay bullish into year end. We expect continued strong earnings 
delivery, and note EPS revisions have moved outright positive in all regions - page 22. This is aided by a rebound in PMIs, 
Eurozone PMIs in particular are up for 3 months in a row, and CESIs remain strong – page 34. 
◼
On the other side, we do not expect rising bond yields to present an insurmountable obstacle for stocks, as most of the upmove 
should be reflecting stronger activity momentum. Term premia has largely normalized, and we do not see inflation expectations 
becoming de-anchored, as wage growth is soft – page 24, and labour markets mixed – page 27 => central banks might deliver 
less tightening than is currently priced in.
◼
Finally, we looked for USD peaking, and for gold to bounce after 20-25% weakness since the Iran conflict started. USD 
positioning remains elevated – page 29, and rates & activity differentials are narrowing. This might even allow the pressure on 
JPY to reduce. In the least, weaker USD typically helps International stocks to perform. We note that Low Vol groups are 
back to lows – page 19, with Staples, Utilities etc relatives faltering. We stay with the view that Tech/AI is unlikely to be the 
dominant driver of returns in 2H, in contrast to 2025, and are looking for continued broadening – page 19.
◼
We have also consistently argued since 2nd half of March to use any equity weakness brought on by the Iran conflict to 
buy into. The flare ups are continuing, and we might see a proverbial “escalation in order to de-escalate”, but the impact on oil 
might get more muted, supply is likely to be structurally higher, and if one joins selling during derisking episodes, one risks being 
whipsawed on any favourable headline.
◼
At the overall index level, we looked for fresh highs for equities in 2H, and call for further gains. Many argued a cautious 
stance into summer, expecting a slowdown. The pain trade is therefore higher, and we find that overall investor positioning is still 
light – page 9. We continue to see big differences to the 2022 template. In terms of broader equity drivers:
◼
1) Fundamentally, Growth-Policy trade-off was supportive ahead of the conflict, and we do not believe that this will be 
dramatically changing.
◼
2) Rate increase expectations are unlikely to get more hawkish. Yield curve has recently flattened again, but this might not 
continue. USD has been acting as a safe haven during the geopolitical escalation, but does not look cheap, and could resume 
depreciating thereafter - page 29. Gold was everyone’s favourite long in January, only to become shunned as it fell back 20-25%. 
We continue to think this is an opportunity to add again, looking for debasement trade to resume.
◼
3) Earnings are coming in strong, and show broadening participation. We believe they will stay a support for the equity market.
◼
4) Valuations still look stretched in the US at 20x forward P/E, but international markets appear cheaper.
◼
5) Technicals in crowded areas have improved, overall equity positioning is rather light, in our view.


[Page 4]
4
…USD is turning lower - this typically helps International stocks; Non-US 
equities are faring well, ahead of the US for 2nd year in a row…
◼
In 2025, MSCI AC World ex US was ahead of the US by a big 15%+, total return, in USD. Despite the Iran conflict, which 
helped US relative performance to pick up during Q2, non-US stocks are again ahead this year, by 4%, If we are right over 
continued broadening in market participation into year end, the Iran conflict not dramatically re-escalating, and questions with 
respect to AI monetisation not going away, the chances are that non-US stocks will finish 2nd year in a row ahead of the US.
◼
OW EM vs DM. After years of our cautious EM stance, we turned bullish on EM in 2025, and think a more positive backdrop 
will remain the case for this year. We expect better EM performance on stabilisation in memory trade post the recent sell-
off, likely reduced trade headwinds vs China, on potentially stronger EM FX and Fed not getting more hawkish into year end, 
with China green shoots/policy stimulus a wildcard. This comes after years of very weak relative EM equity performance, 
which coincided with USD strength – page 31. EM still look cheap and under-owned, with clear inflows potential – page 32. 
Our EM team is bullish on Korea, China, Taiwan, Chile and South Africa, among other.
◼
Eurozone was a relative loser from the conflict, and was hampered while AI staged a rebound in Q2. Having said that, we see 
better trading in 2H. Eurozone earnings were outright down again in 2025, as we feared, but we think that region’s corporate 
profits are set to rebound this year. A broadening in leadership, where Value, small caps, Cyclicals and exporters join the rally, 
should be seen, provided that the conflict doesn’t reignite in 2H. Eurozone PMIs were understandably weaker during the initial 
stages of Iran war, but should keep rebounding. Encouragingly, Eurozone credit growth and car sales have been 
improving, PMIs are up for 3 months in a row, as are EPS revisions. Positioning has become as low as seen in January 
of ‘25 - page 34.
◼
Japan’s outlook fundamentally continues to look strong, but it remains a hostage to Tech direction and valuations are elevated 
– page 51, with risks over increased JPY and JGB volatility. On the positive side, TSE reforms are continuing, with buybacks 
at new highs and wage growth is accelerating. The tail risk is that loose fiscal policy could lead to a runaway JPY depreciation 
and/or more significant bond yields spike, which would be a negative for equities, and China-Japan tensions could simmer. 
This is not to say that there won’t be further absolute gains for Japan. Within Japan, we are long Banks, as the gap with 
yields has widened – page 51.
◼
US activity remains robust. The question is over the still relatively elevated investor positioning, together with full valuations 
and the Tech/AI trade potentially seeing continued volatility. We do not see Tech driving returns in 2H, unlike what was the 
case last summer.
◼
The UK is showing a large valuation discount vs other regions, as well as the highest dividend yield globally, and is to a good 
extent immune to both the AI cannibalisation and AI unwind trades. Also, UK is comparatively less trade sensitive, and is one 
of the very good places to hide during the risk-off episodes.
◼
Big picture, the starting point between US and International equity weights, as well as valuation divergence, remain substantial 
– page 47.


[Page 5]
5
…low beta sectors such as Staples and Utilities are faltering again; Mining, 
Banks and Capital Goods to keep rallying, Semis to keep stabilizing
◼
In terms of Tech/AI trade, we remain of the view that it will struggle to dominate in 2H, unlike what transpired in 2025. Q2 
breadth was too narrow, momentum factor too hot, Mag-7 are likely to keep experiencing ROI anxiety and we stay cautious on 
AI cannibalization areas – Software, Business Services and Media. Having said that, the derating and underperformance of 
the group was exceptional in 1H already, with some tactical bounces likely. Semis remain a cyclical trade, and here the June-
July selloff has likely corrected the positioning excess, with earnings/capex uptrend that is unlikely to change anytime soon. 
Our pecking order into year end is Semis over hyperscalers over AI at risk plays.
◼
In terms of beta, if we are right that inflation fears ease, curve re-steepens, and that PMIs bounce back from the conflict driven 
softening, then beta sectors should be performing well in 2H. We entered the year long Cyclicals, in the June-July 
looked for some Low-Vol tactical bounce, but believe Cyclicals will lead again into year end – page 19. This could even 
include Consumer Cyclicals. The group was a big laggard for a long while - page 41. Interestingly, when consumer 
confidence is at lows, such as most recently, consumer groups tended to outperform over the next 12 months – page 
42. This included Homebuilders, Airlines, Retail, Hotels, Luxury and Autos. Big picture, Consumer Cyclicals remain in the eye 
of the storm, with profit warnings and cautious guidance, but they could see better performance ahead.
◼
Thematically, the Defense sector was not working for more than 12 months, and we recently argued to turn more 
positive on the space – page 18. We advised at end of September ‘25 to reduce Defense on likely rotation, but now think 
one could re-enter the group, favouring modernization plays. Stylewise, likely broadening in market participation should mean 
better performance of Small caps – page 44. In Europe, Value performed well last year– page 43, and we look for more this 
year.
◼
In terms of European sectoral positioning, we believe the outlook for capex/electrification is still constructive, supporting 
Industrials. Within Financials, Banks fundamentally look attractive, and benefit from the potential acceleration in activity that 
we look for this year, including a pickup in credit growth. We have taken profits on Insurance last year on weaker pricing, and 
have a pair trade in Financials of Banks over Insurance. We remain unexcited about Defensives, relatively cautious on 
Staples, Real Estate, Telecoms and Healthcare, but Utilities look interesting, and we remain positive on Renewables.
◼
We are positive on Basic Resources, having double upgraded the sector in March ’25. We think the Q2 weakness is 
another opportunity to add, as the gap with spot metal prices has reopened – page 50. The wild card is the peak in USD and 
general growth acceleration. Energy stocks do not look attractive unless Iran conflict fully re-escalates, in our view, given 
likely increased supply, driven by UAE, Colombia, Iraq and Venezuela changes. There is no cushion between Energy equities 
and the spot evident – page 50.
◼
Out of key themes, we highlight: Eurozone Fiscal Stimulus – JPEZFIST, Ukraine-Russia – JPEUUKRN, Trade Uncertainty 
names – JPEUTFS, which might have been penalised too much, winners from potential easing in oil prices –JPDEOILL, and 
AI cannibalisation basket - JPEUAIL.

Regional Allocation: 1) OW EM vs DM – we turned bullish EM last year, after 
our multi year cautious stance…
◼
EM equities have outperformed DM in 2025 after cumulatively 
underperforming DM by almost 50% between 2018 and 2024. We 
took advantage of this multi-year underperformance to upgrade 
EM to outright OW in May ‘25, this follows our upgrade to Neutral 
back in March ‘25. 
◼
Notably, EM has held up versus DM this year.
◼
Within EM, Asia is one of the most sensitive regions to energy 
flows from Middle East, but we note that Asian oil inventories 
range from 4-8 months 
◼
If the Fed resumes its dovish bias, this backdrop has historically 
favoured EM outperformance over DM.

… if the conflict fades and Fed resumes its dovish bias, EM central banks will

◼

The recent weakness in EM AI hardware names could be a tactical opportunity
Source: J.P. Morgan, IBES
Source: J.P. Morgan, Bloomberg Finance L.P.
◼
Despite the recent selloff, the Asia AI Hardware basket has 
outperformed the Mag-7 complex by over 70% since the start 
of 2025, and we see scope of a rebound as AI hardware 
benefits from strong spending trends from the hyperscalers.
◼
The Asia Tech hardware ecosystem sits at the centre of the 
global AI buildout, and our colleagues see the supply demand 
tightness persisting for at least the next two to three quarters, 
supporting continued pricing power across the chain. 
◼
Critically, AI-related names represent a substantial portion of 
the overall EM index, accounting for approximately 40% of 
EM Asia market capitalisation. 
◼
Notably, valuations do appear rich for the group though.

The worst of the trade uncertainty appears to be behind us
Source: Bloomberg Finance L.P.
Source: J.P. Morgan
Fiscal support for consumption (bn yuan)
Source: J.P. Morgan China Economists
JPM China forecast revision index
US – China Effective Tariff rate
◼
We encounter considerable skepticism about China’s growth 
outlook, with most investors not expecting material fiscal support 
from policymakers. We see scope for a positive surprise: our 
economists believe pressure is mounting for additional support, 
especially as domestic demand needs stimulus given limited 
appetite among corporates and households to take on more 
leverage. Other considerations include technology upgrades and 
indigenization, and support for local government finances that are 
strained by weak land sales and tax revenues amid a prolonged 
housing correction and subdued nominal GDP growth. They 
expect a greater emphasis on consumption-focused measures, 
including trade-in subsidies, pension increases, birth incentives, 
and childcare support. The 15th Five Year Plan (FYP) calls for a 
“notable increase in the consumption rate.”

The wild card is if China domestic activity surprises on the upside
Source: J.P. Morgan China Economists
Source: Bloomberg Finance L.P.
China M1 and M2
Source: J.P. Morgan China Economists
China fiscal policy stance
China Land sales revenue
◼
The housing slowdown was one of the drivers of our long-held 
cautious view on Chinese activity. However, we believe that we 
may be close to an inflection point. While activity may not ramp up 
to levels seen pre-Covid, any stabilization should go a long way 
towards getting investors comfortable with owning the region again. 
◼
Domestic liquidity measures have been improving, with both M1 
and M2 higher. The policy shift since last year is notable, with a 
focus on the “three-arrows”: (1) structural rebalancing (services vs. 
manufacturing, consumption vs. investment); (2) fiscal stimulus; (3) 
monetary easing.
◼
While a fiscal bazooka isn’t imminent, our economists expect 
modest fiscal expansion, and a focus on structural issues like the 
recent anti-involution policy and boosting domestic demand / 
consumption.

Within EM, our EM strategists are constructive on China, Taiwan, Korea, 
Malaysia, Chile, Poland, South Africa and Peru…
◼
China – Advanced manufacturing, technology upgrade, and global expansion drive earnings 
growth. Strong exports despite subdued domestic consumption and housing markets. 
Relatively resilient in the face of higher energy prices. Improved liquidity and sentiment from 
tech trade, increased retail participation, Stock Connect, and global diversification. Continued 
policy support: fiscal and monetary easing, anti-involution measures, and governance reforms.
◼
Taiwan -  A proxy for global AI capex, Taiwan has upstream and midstream firms in the AI 
supply chain that are benefiting from global AI investment. Strong management confidence 
and easing AI monetization concerns support sentiment despite stretched valuations and 
concentration risk.
◼
Korea - A multi-year upcycle under for memory stocks, with earnings visibility improved by 
LTAs. Strong EPS growth also expected in non-memory sectors (financials, holdcos, and 
select industrials). Cheap relative to its historical average or global peers. Re-rating expected 
with further governance reforms. Additional upside may come from wealth effect, earnings 
delivery and repatriation flow despite implementation challenges and technical correction risks. 
◼
Malaysia – Political noise may persist as electoral cycles approach. Any populist tilt is likely to 
be consumer-supportive, rather than policy-disruptive. Malaysia continues to strengthen its 
investment proposition through export competitiveness initiatives, policy reform execution, and 
resilient domestic and tourism-driven consumption. Limited risk from high oil prices as a net 
energy exporter.
◼
Chile - The recently approved Reconstruction Bill presents an ambitious reform package 
including lower corporate tax, tax invariability for investment project, and faster environmental 
permitting should help boost investment.Implementation progress is key to watch. Supportive 
copper prices is another tailwind, but it is also critical that oil prices come down/stabilize as 
Chile as 100% oil import dependence.
◼
Poland – GDP still strong. Strong 2Q earnings season. Should outperform EM if EUR can 
rebound post-conflict. 
◼
South Africa – Best structural reform story in CEEMEA. Gold/PGM stocks should be 
rebounding now. ZAR to rally if oil ticks lower and Fed hike fears recede.
◼
Peru - We see the beginning of a favourable investment cycle from better political stability. A 
pro-investment stance could help unlock capital spending. Also, the mix of strong metal prices 
and declining oil is supportive for Peru’s macro, and the robust copper project pipeline should 
be a medium-term tailwind for growth.

2) Overweight Eurozone - upgraded our target for additional upside in late 
June
◼
Eurozone equity markets had been more or less flat between 
March ‘25 and September ‘25, underperforming other DM 
benchmarks over the time frame. We took advantage of this 
relative underperformance to upgrade the region to OW in 
October. Eurozone and EM are more reliant on energy imports and 
therefore have been more vulnerable during the conflict. We 
believe while these indices could see more downside risk if the 
conflict escalates, we would use it as an opportunity to buy as their 
fundamental drivers remain intact, and as oil potentially reverses. 
We upgraded the targets further in late June.

Our bullish outlook on European earnings is showing traction
◼
The key driver behind our constructive view on European 
equities is the earnings outlook, which appears set to 
improve meaningfully this year, after 3 subdued years.
◼
Some of the elevated projected growth can be attributed to 
base effects in the Discretionary sector. Even ex 
Discretionary, Eurozone earnings are growing at a healthy 
+12% y/y.
◼
Encouragingly, the breadth of European earnings upgrades 
has also been improving. Eurozone EPS revisions have 
been inflecting higher over past few months.

[Page 91]

The excessive German stimulus optimism from Q1 ‘25 has faded, just at the 
time when spending could show an uplift
◼
Germany (and much of Europe) spent decades under-investing 
in physical infrastructure: public capex lagged the euro-area 
average and replacement needs, leaving net public capital 
broadly flat/declining through the 2010s and creating a backlog 
across transport, schools, plus weaker digital and grid capacity. 
This is a multi-year catch-up, not a cyclical top-up.
◼
Fiscal policy is now turning: the structural primary deficit is set to 
widen from -0.6% of GDP (2025) to -2.2% (2026) and -2.6% 
(2027)—~2% of GDP easing over two years—while the total 
federal deficit (incl. Special Funds) stays near ~4% of GDP 
through 2030. Markets have faded the stimulus trade since Q1, 
but the €500bn infrastructure fund is real; the issue has been 
timing/planning. By June 2026, the cumulative federal deficit was 
already €12bn wider y/y, about half the full-year widening implied 
by the 2026 budget.
Source: J.P.Morgan
Source: J.P.Morgan

Defense spending is picking up… Infrastructure is likely to move higher, too
◼
For years, Europe underinvested in readiness, inventories, 
munitions, air defence, and sustainment. The Russia-Ukraine 
war changed the policy baseline. Many European NATO 
members spent below the 2% of GDP guideline. The post-Cold-
War "peace dividend" saw real defence budgets drift lower over 
decades, munitions stockpiles were run down faster than they 
were replenished, and equipment readiness across several 
fleets sat at low availability. Procurement was fragmented 
across national programs, limiting scale and slowing capacity 
build. 
◼
Rising NATO spending commitments and EU-level joint 
procurement, including ReArm Europe and EDIP-type 
programs and common munitions replenishment contracts, 
provide a durable demand backdrop for the sector.

Within Eurozone, we think that peripheral outperformance vs core, that took 
place over the past few years, could wane
◼
Within Eurozone, we see better prospects for Core 
(Germany, France) vs Periphery (Italy, Spain). Core 
equity markets have meaningfully lagged Periphery 
again since Jan ’25, and we believe that one should 
position for a reversal going forward.
◼
Periphery benefitted from a strong Banks rally, and 
while we believe Banks look attractive for 2026, their 
outperformance could subside.
◼
The relative performance of Periphery over core has 
been supported by better earnings delivery and may 
struggle if the relative earnings momentum slows.

[Page 99]
99
India – EU trade deal
Source: JPMorgan
◼
The European Union and India recently concluded a free trade agreement. In 2024, bilateral trade in goods between the two partners 
amounted to €120 billion, with EU imports from India totaling €71 billion and EU exports to India reaching nearly €49 billion.
◼
Under the agreement, the EU agreed to eliminate tariffs on over 90% of tariff lines, covering 91% of trade by value, while India agreed 
to eliminate tariffs on 86% of tariff lines, covering 93% of trade by value. Both sides also partially liberalised a significant number of 
additional tariff lines, bringing the overall coverage of trade liberalisation to 96.6% for India and 99.3% for the EU.
◼
Key economic sectors benefited from the agreement: For the EU, these include agri-food, chemicals, pharmaceuticals, machinery, 
medical devices, avionics, and automotive industries. For India, the main beneficiaries are fisheries, chemicals, textiles, footwear, and 
pharmaceuticals.
India EU trade deal impact on sectors
Sector
Impact
Autos and motor vehicles
Tariffs on EU motor vehicles cut from 110% to 10% (with a 250k quota), materially improving price competitiveness and 
access to India 
Machinery and electrical equipment (Capital Goods)
Tariffs (previously up to 44%) reduced to 0% for almost all products
Binding of dredging and maritime cable-laying services for the first time, directly benefiting EU firms in these niche
Chemicals
Tariffs (up to 22%) cut to 0% for almost all products, largely at entry into force, boosting EU chemical producers’ 
competitiveness 
Pharmaceuticals, medical equipment
Tariffs (11%) reduced to 0% for almost all products
Tariffs (up to 27.5%) cut to 0% for 90% of products, aiding EU medtech and diagnostic equipment exporters 
Plastics
Tariffs (up to 16.5%) reduced to 0% for almost all products, supporting EU plastics and materials companies 
Iron and steel (Mining)
Tariffs (up to 22%) cut to 0% for almost all products
Aircraft and spacecraft (Aerospace)
Tariffs (up to 11%) reduced to 0% for almost all products within up to 10 years (mostly 5)
Pearls, precious stones, and metals 
0% tariffs for 20% of products and tariff reductions for another 36%, aiding European jewelry and precious metals sectors 
Services: financial and maritime transport
Privileged access to India’s services market in key areas (e.g., financial services, maritime transport) and simplification of 
customs procedures, improving EU service providers’ operating environment 
Food and beverages 
Reduced or removed tariffs on agricultural exports such as wine and olive oil; preferential access for wines, spirits, beers, 
confectionery, and other EU food products to the fast-growing Indian market.

4) Neutral UK - it lacks clear medium-term catalysts…
◼
UK equities tended to perform like a defensive market 
historically, and are unlikely to lead if risk assets resume 
advancing.
◼
Looking at the past correlations, the UK has traditionally been 
a low-beta, Defensive market, which performs well on a 
relative basis during downturns, rather than during periods of 
improving growth-policy tradeoffs.
◼
Periods of clear UK equity outperformance tended to coincide 
with global shocks.

…and it offers the highest dividend yield of any of the big DMs
Source: Bloomberg Finance L.P.
Source: IBES
◼
From a total return perspective, the UK offers a much 
higher dividend yield compared to other regions, 
even excluding commodity sectors.
◼
Dividend yield looks attractive, and the payout ratio is 
still meaningfully below average. In addition, dividend 
strategies are likely to gain traction if our fixed 
income team’s view that bond yields move lower is 
vindicated. 
◼
UK has a high proportion of income funds and could 
benefit as investors turn to dividend strategies.

5) Neutral US – activity is robust, with labour market softness…
◼
We are constructive on U.S. equities in absolute 
terms, but the question is over the concentration 
risk and the valuation premium.
◼
U.S. activity so far has been fairly resilient, 
despite the faltering labour market. Geopolitical 
headwinds do not appear to have majorly 
impacted activity momentum so far.

Mag-7 stocks were the primary contributor to US equity performance over the 
last decade
◼
A significant portion of the US performance can be 
attributed to Mag-7.This performance was supported 
by exceptionally strong earnings growth. The 
earnings of the Mag-7 group have increased at a 
CAGR of 20% since 2010, compared to a 7% 
CAGR for the remainder of S&P 500.
◼
U.S. earnings have continued to dominate last year.

Investor positioning is high…US households have close to record levels of 
equities on their balance sheet
Source: Bloomberg Finance L.P.
Source: Federal Reserve, J.P. Morgan
US household ownership of stocks, % of total assets
◼
We are also concerned about the elevated level of investor positioning in the US.
◼
This is not confined just to the institutional investor; US households also have close to record levels of 
equities on their balance sheet. This in turn has resulted in $8 trillion in extra cash for households last year, 
and this positive wealth effect is likely to support the consumer.

3) Defense champions basket
◼
Our basket of European defense plays includes 
stocks that are significantly exposed to the 
European defense sector. The basket includes 
firms that stand to benefit from a structural shift in 
government policy, as European countries move 
to reverse decades of under-investment in 
defense and security. After a multi-year positive 
view on the Defence sector, we turned cautious 
last summer as the theme became crowded. With 
Defence having derated meaningfully, we believe 
the risk/reward for the group will turn better going 
forward.

◼

that are strategically positioned to benefit from a potential 
ceasefire in Ukraine and the subsequent rebuilding 
process. The basket focuses on firms that could 
experience an increase in demand for their products and 
services as peace returns and reconstruction efforts begin. 
The basket aims to capture the upside from both direct 
participation in rebuilding Ukraine and the broader 
economic improvement that could follow. The D1 Team 
compiled the basket based on inputs from European 
Equity Research, Spec Sales and D1 Sensitivity analysis.

from European Equity Research sector analysts.

◼
Oil prices have rebounded on renewed escalation in the U.S.–
Iran conflict. However, any meaningful de-escalation would 
bring oil prices back toward pre-crisis levels or even lower. The 
key risk here is that Energy equities have re-priced almost 
entirely in lockstep with Brent, leaving virtually no valuation 
buffer to absorb a pullback in crude.
◼
Our Commodities team sees Gulf supply normalizing more 
slowly than assumed. Commercial flows are running at just 50% 
(11.1mbd) including 7.0 mbd of vulnerable pipeline re-routing 
amid a reported Houthi Red Sea blockade. The Strait remains a 
vital chokepoint, with approximately 20% of the world's oil 
supply and a significant share of global LNG transiting through 
its waters. 
◼
Their research suggests that, inventories have drawn deeper 
than April forecasts and should further deplete through August 
before. pivoting to a modest build (+0.4 mbd) only from 
September, with larger surpluses in Q4. However, a full rebuild 
is constrained: ~500 mn bbl is already drawn, with a further 
~210–500 mn bbl needed if disruptions persist, from a pool 
nearing operational limits

OW Mining – stronger China outlook and potential return to weaker USD to 
help…
◼
We maintained a cautious stance on Metals for a long 
time, in line with our conservative outlook on EM and 
China. However, in Mar ‘25, we saw an opportunity and 
double upgraded the sector. The sector has reversed 
some of its conflict-driven gains, but we expect it to 
continue performing well.
◼
A potential improvement in domestic activity in China 
may serve as the next catalyst for the group. 
◼
Miners stand to gain from improved trading in 
underlying commodity prices, particularly if the dollar 
weakens further.

◼
Following the pullback in March, copper prices have 
rebounded again. This has been supported by robust 
buying activity from China. Our analysts still expect 
global copper demand to outstrip supply this year, with 
the resolution of ongoing mine disruptions likely pushed 
into 2027.

copper inventory to the lowest level during this period in 
a decade.
◼
Mining sector performance has opened up a gap with 
copper prices again. Last year, supply disruptions at 
Freeport’s Grasberg mine, the world’s second-largest 
copper mine, drove copper prices materially higher.

◼
European cement stocks have come under significant pressure 
this year as political uncertainty and headlines about potential 
EU ETS reforms have triggered investor panic over the future 
of carbon pricing and the sector’s ability to maintain profitability. 
Additionally, the Iran conflict has added to broader market 
volatility, further weighing on investor sentiment.
◼
Still, margins for the sector have stayed strong.
◼
Within the group, Lightside (non cement building products) has 
not fared as well, particularly as both residential and non-
residential construction activity has been subdued.
◼
We note that the group trades expensive but believe it will be 
supported by activity rebound and the fiscal support coming 
through.

◼
The Chemicals sector has been struggling for a number of years 
now and has fallen a further 20% relative to the market since Jan 
‘25.
◼
The sector remains sensitive to developments in China, where port 
inventories have generally stayed tight versus last year, even if 
periodic import arrivals have caused short-term builds in some 
products. Expectations still lean toward further inventory 
drawdowns into August as imports remain relatively limited (partly 
linked to Middle East disruption risk),
◼
Our sector analysts believe that, while many companies have 
announced substantial price increases to pass through higher raw 
material and energy costs and, where possible, to expand margins, 
acceptance and demand elasticity remain key unknowns.
◼
Fertilizers are seen to be one of the key beneficiaries of the 
ongoing US-Iran conflict.

INDUSTRIALS: OVERWEIGHT
OW Capital Goods – Strong earnings and record high margins driven by AI 
and electrification…
◼
The Capital Goods sector has delivered strong gains last 
year, supported by resilient earnings, continued electrification 
theme and A&D exposure, with this momentum continuing in 
2026 as well, before the conflict erupted. We believe this 
pullback offers an attractive entry point for investors. 
◼
The strongest growth in 2026 is again expected to be in 
datacenters and utilities, which are major end-markets for 
Capital Goods. These sectors benefit from secular trends 
such as AI, electrification, digitization and the transition to net-
zero.
◼
This should help sustain record margins for the sector.

Source: Datastream
OW Aerospace: Entering harvesting phase with strong EPS growth and robust 
aftermarket demand
◼
The industry is entering what analysts describe as a "harvesting 
phase" - a period with no major new aircraft programs in 
development, which lowers execution risk and allows margins to 
expand as existing platforms mature. 
◼
EPS growth for the major European civil aerospace companies is 
forecast in the mid-to-high-teens over the next several years. 
Aftermarket revenues, are expected to grow around 10% annually - 
a moderation from recent peaks but still robust. With aircraft 
retirements not anticipated to accelerate until late 2027, aftermarket 
demand and pricing power for spare parts should remain resilient. 
Our analysts see significant downside risks to consensus forecasts. 
◼
Supply chain bottlenecks have created significant backlogs for 
OEMs, ensuring sustained demand for both new aircraft deliveries 
and aftermarket services. Additionally, the defence divisions of these 
aerospace companies stand to benefit from the structural increase in 
European defence budgets, adding another layer of earnings 
visibility and diversification.

Source: Datastream
N Defense
◼
Within Capital Goods, we have been very bullish on the 
Aerospace & Defense subgroup over the past two years. The 
Aerospace & Defense sector had outperformed the index by 
more than 90% from 2024 to its peak in Q3 ‘25 amid increased 
geopolitical tensions and as a beneficiary of rising defense 
spending. Post this move, we turned more cautious on 
defense.
◼
However, since then, Defense has derated meaningfully, and 
we believe the risk/reward for the group will turn better going 
forward. 
◼
Our preference, though, is for new capabilities rather than 
legacy platforms, i.e. drones, electronic warfare, munitions 
replenishment, and space, rather than legacy warships or 
heavy armoured vehicles. We would use the de-rating as an 
opportunity to rebuild exposure selectively rather than chase 
the sector wholesale.

N Transport
◼
The sector has seen some correction from its highs in July, as jet 
fuel prices have started to increase again.
◼
Looking ahead, our sector analysts expect air traffic growth to 
pick up in the near term. Air traffic in the Middle East had been 
disrupted as the conflict involving Iran raised security risks 
prompting temporary airspace restrictions, flight suspensions, and 
widespread rerouting that increased flight times and reduced 
capacity through key corridors, but we believe the region can 
recover quickly if conditions remain stable. Within the sector, our 
analysts prefer European flag carriers over low-cost carriers, as 
flag carriers are benefiting from capacity discipline and strong 
long-haul/premium demand, enabling higher fuel-cost pass-
through (60% guidance for Q2).

The performance of the European Autos sector has

sector has fallen more than 50% relative to the broader

uncertainty is likely already priced in.

…structural concerns remain but resilient pricing and declining discounts are

Source: JPM European Autos Research Team
Source: JPM European Autos Research Team
Source: JPM European Autos Research Team
Weighted average discounts across Europe
Chinese OEMs market share and volume in Europe
◼
The sector is grappling with structural headwinds, including 
substantial costs associated with the transition to electric 
vehicles and fierce competition from Chinese OEMs. European 
automakers’ share of China’s premium vehicle market has 
dropped markedly in recent years, while Chinese 
manufacturers continue to capture a growing share of the 
European market.
◼
There are some encouraging signs, with pricing power showing 
resilience across European markets. Dealer consolidation is 
also creating early signs of price stabilisation: German 
premium OEMs have reduced dealer networks by ~20% vs 
2024, which should improve inventory control and support 
profitability. Discounts have been steadily declining since their 
peak in 2Q19.

OW Consumer Durables/Luxury – Korea growth and China recovery with

◼
European Luxury had underperformed the market by 25% till 
May, before stabilizing since then. Soft demand from Chinese

engine, with Korea now representing a larger exposure than 
the Middle East. Retail sales growth there remains strong.

macro environment stabilizes.

CONSUMER STAPLES: UNDERWEIGHT  
 
 
           
Staples struggle with weak pricing power and demand
Source: Datastream
◼
European Staples have underperformed the broader market 
for several years due to weak demand and stagnant volumes.
◼
As a result, valuations for the sector have derated to 2 
standard deviations cheap relative to the broader market.
◼
With geopolitical uncertainty in the Middle East pushing up 
energy and input costs, and the Staples COGS tracker now 
signalling a renewed risk of cost inflation for 2026/27. Sector 
analysts expect cost pressures to intensify for HPC and 
Beverages, likely to weigh on margins.

FINANCIALS: NEUTRAL
N Banks – Valuation re-rating drive outperformance, and balance sheets 
remain strong…
◼
European Banks have delivered impressive returns since Jan 
‘25 and are up almost 55% on the back of fading geopolitical 
risks, anticipation of easing regulatory reform and M&A 
activity. We believe the sector could continue to trade well 
even with a stabilization in interest rates. 
◼
Valuations remain attractive in a historical context and are 
supported by improving ROE trends in the sector.
◼
The sector’s balance sheets have steadily improved in recent 
years, accompanied by a reduction in non-performing loans. 
According to our Banks team, the sector is capable of 
absorbing loan loss provisions exceeding 250bp. Even under 
Basel 4, they project a CET1 ratio of 14%.

Source: IBES, Worldscope

UW Insurance – Margins to be under pressure as premiums drop and spreads 
widen
◼
Following a strong start to 2025, the European 
Insurance sector underperformed the broader index 
from April ‘25 into the May lows by almost 15%, before 
recovering in the last few weeks. We think the sector’s 
underperformance is likely to continue.
◼
The sector had benefited from the strength in credit 
markets, but this support may diminish if spreads 
begin to widen.
◼
After a period of significant premium hikes, particularly 
in motor insurance, the market is showing signs of 
softening. Insurance premiums have fallen even as 
cost inflation pertaining to claims has been relatively 
elevated, threatening insurer margins.

REAL ESTATE: NEUTRAL
Bond proxy; cheap valuations should support the sector…
◼
European Real Estate continues to face significant challenges, 
driven by volatile global macroeconomic conditions, inflation 
pressures particularly in the UK and expectations of further rate 
hikes by the Bank of England. The shift from anticipated rate 
cuts to potential rate increases has intensified the difficult 
environment for real estate investments, as higher borrowing 
costs and tighter financial conditions weigh on asset values and 
investor sentiment.
◼
The sector is a clear bond proxy and is affected by rising bond 
yields. 
◼
Valuations for the sector appear supportive.

TECHNOLOGY: NEUTRAL
Tech sector has struggled recently, despite the strong earnings
◼
European Tech peaked in June at multi-year highs, almost 50% 
rally from September lows. The surge was driven by strong 
earnings, renewed optimism around AI and cloud technologies, and 
positive momentum from global tech leaders.
◼
Since then, the sector has corrected by 10% over the past three 
months as investors have grown more cautious on the outlook, 
citing potential overbuild, questions around future hyperscaler 
capital spending, and uncertainty on the timing of incremental 
demand for AI chips and memory.
◼
Within European Tech, Semis have seen profit booking following a 
strong rally since September, while Software has underperformed 
throughout this period.
◼
Global Tech sector earnings have been strong.

OW Semis – Semis to outperform on AI demand and DRAM price surge
◼
Semis had outperformed the market by almost 150% from the 
summer 2025 lows, supported by strong earnings momentum, 
before giving back some gains more recently amid profit booking.
◼
Interestingly, an increasing gap is opening between Semis price 
relative and earnings relative, where Semis stocks have rolled over 
despite continued earnings resilience. With fundamentals staying 
strong, we think that investors should add to the space over the 
summer.
◼
Our sector analyst highlights that ASML, which makes up 80% of the 
European Semis index, is a significant beneficiary of the ongoing 
robust upcycle in the memory market, particularly driven by strong AI 
demand, elevated DRAM prices and continued capacity build out at 
TSMC: they note the larger than expected step up in 2026 capex 
announced recently.
◼
European Semis have high exposure to Consumer end market, that 
is expected to see a pickup. 
Source: Bloomberg Finance L.P.

UW Software & Services – headwinds from AI drive business models shifts
Source: IBES
MSCI Europe Software constituents and YTD performance
Source: Datastream
MSCI Europe Software relative
Source: IBES
MSCI Europe Software 12m Fwd EPS relative
◼
Software had an extremely poor performance since Jan 
‘25 underperforming the market by more than 50% till its 
lows in July. Post the derating, the sector has seen better 
trading on the back of accelerating trends by SAP and 
SAGE. 
◼
Despite the underperformance, the sector saw strong 
earnings upgrades in 2025, but earnings momentum has 
started to roll over.
◼
The sector is seen to be a potential loser of the AI drive, 
as this could disrupt traditional software business 
models, lead to more intense competition, and put 
pressure on margins. We do not think that this sentiment 
will change in a hurry.

COMMUNICATION SERVICES: NEUTRAL
N Telecoms – Elevated M&A expectations create an uncertain outlook…
◼
After a strong start to the year, Telecoms sector has lost 
momentum and is now down almost 20% since its high, 
with ongoing uncertainties including regulatory hurdles 
and political risks limiting the potential for further 
upside. Much of the drag has been due to Deutsche 
Telekom, the sector’s largest stock, which is 
underperforming amid competitive pressures in the U.S. 
◼
Our sector analysts believe that capital spending has 
peaked and will start to decline significantly from 2027 
onwards. 
◼
Reduced capital expenditures are helping Telecom 
companies generate much stronger cash flow.
Source: J.P. Morgan European Telecoms Research

[Page 182]
182
UW Media – AI uncertainty is a concern
◼
European Media stocks had a strong rally in the first half 
of 2025 but have since then have corrected by almost 
40%. This is mainly because investors are worried about 
the overall outlook for the sector and are uncertain about 
how AI might affect traditional advertising agencies and 
the music industry in the long run. We worry that these 
concerns will not go away, likely keeping the sector on 
the back foot.
◼
Media has been hurt by persistent earnings downgrades 
and valuations do not appear overly attractive.

UTILITIES: NEUTRAL
Potential for lower bond yields to support the sector in the coming months…
◼
European Utilities have erased their year-to-date 
gains as bond yields have risen. However, we 
believe that the sector is supported by strong 
long-term growth visibility from regulated 
networks and contracted renewables. The sector 
remains a bond proxy and should benefit if bond 
yields move lower from here. 
◼
Cash flow yields are expected to pick up this year.
◼
Earnings momentum remains strong, and Utilities 
remain one of the highest dividend yielding 
sectors in Europe.
Source: Worldscope, IBES

…elevated power prices and geopolitical risks to drive renewables 
investment
◼
Renewables (45% of the European Utilities sector) 
are favoured for their stable, long-life cash flows and 
defensive characteristics. The Middle East conflict 
has reinforced Europe’s push to accelerate 
renewables investment and reduce reliance on 
imported gas. 
◼
Energy-price volatility is likely to persist, keeping 
European power prices elevated. This can benefit 
utilities through increased revenues, but the surge in 
gas price may weigh on margins.

Explanation of Equity Research Ratings, Designations and Analyst(s) Coverage Universe: 
J.P. Morgan uses the following rating system: Overweight (over the duration of the price target indicated in this report, we expect this stock will outperform the average total return 
of the stocks in the Research Analyst’s, or the Research Analyst’s team’s, coverage universe); Neutral (over the duration of the price target indicated in this report, we expect this 
stock will perform in line with the average total return of the stocks in the Research Analyst’s, or the Research Analyst’s team’s, coverage universe); and Underweight (over the 
duration of the price target indicated in this report, we expect this stock will underperform the average total return of the stocks in the Research Analyst’s, or the Research 
Analyst’s team’s, coverage universe. NR is Not Rated. In this case, J.P. Morgan has removed the rating and, if applicable, the price target, for this stock because of either a lack of 
a sufficient fundamental basis or for legal, regulatory or policy reasons. The previous rating and, if applicable, the price target, no longer should be relied upon. An NR designation 
is not a recommendation or a rating. Some stocks under coverage have a rating but no price target; in these cases, we expect the stock will outperform/perform in 
line/underperform the average total return of the stocks in the Research Analyst’s, or the Research Analyst’s team’s, coverage universe of the relevant duration of the region. In our 
Asia (ex-Australia and ex-India) and U.K. small- and mid-cap Equity Research, each stock’s expected total return is compared to the expected total return of a benchmark country 
market index, not to those Research Analysts’ coverage universe. If it does not appear in the Important Disclosures section of this report, the certifying Research Analyst’s 
coverage universe can be found on J.P. Morgan’s Research website, https://www.jpmorganmarkets.com.
